========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, hello Doctor. Thank you so much for having me. I, um, I hope I'm not taking up too much of your time with this. It's probably silly, but... well, I came in because I just can't seem to get anything in order anymore. 

It's a bit embarrassing to admit, honestly. Like, my kitchen table is just covered right now with my husband's appointment papers, and church flyers, and notes from the senior center... oh, and some half-finished lists of mine. I sit there, meaning to sort it all out, but I just don't know which thing to do first. And then somehow the whole day is just gone. I feel awful about it, because I really should be able to manage a simple table, shouldn't I? Is that... is that the sort of thing I should be telling you?

Well, I suppose... I mean, I've always been a little bit of a daydreamer, maybe not the most perfectly tidy person in the world, but it was never really a bother. But it really started getting to be, well, a bit too much for me to handle about a year and three months ago.

I wanted to do it all perfectly for him, of course, because he's been so good to me. But suddenly it was just... so many papers and dates, and that's when things really started piling up on the table. It wasn't overnight, but it felt like once I took on his schedule, I just couldn't keep my head above water. I feel terrible even saying that, because I should be able to care for my husband without making a mess of things, shouldn't I? I hope I don't sound like I'm complaining about him... it's just my own failing, really. Does that timeline help you at all?

for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve missed returning calls, left bills sitting under a stack of mail, forgotten errands I promised my daughter I’d do, and more than once realized too late that I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: Even when I’m trying to sit politely in a meeting, I catch myself rubbing my hands, tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around so much that people look over at me.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: During volunteer meetings or even at supper, I keep getting up to check the calendar, look for a paper, refill water, or just move around, even when I know I’m supposed to stay seated and listen.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even as a little girl, my teachers wrote that I was bright but careless, forgot books and homework, and needed reminders to finish seatwork before I could go outside.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I had to give up leading a big senior-center event, and my daughter has started double-checking bills and appointments because she no longer trusts that I can keep everything straight on my own.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: It has been causing this much trouble for about a year and three months, since my husband started needing regular help with his appointments and daily arrangements.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: Oh, hello Doctor. Thank you so much for having me. I try to stay quite cheerful, actually! \n\nIt's really just... well, just this silliness with not being able to keep my own house in order. I certainly don't want to give you the impression that I'm ungrateful or depressed. I'm just a little frustrated with myself, mostly, for being so scattered. I hope that makes sense? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information... did I answer that okay?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you started having trouble organizing and keeping track of things like you're describing? Was it gradual, or did it come on more suddenly?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I've always been a little bit of a daydreamer, maybe not the most perfectly tidy person in the world, but it was never really a bother. But it really started getting to be, well, a bit too much for me to handle about a year and three months ago. \n\nThat was when my husband's health changed a bit, and he started needing regular help keeping track of his appointments and all his daily arrangements. I wanted to do it all perfectly for him, of course, because he's been so good to me. But suddenly it was just... so many papers and dates, and that's when things really started piling up on the table. It wasn't overnight, but it felt like once I took on his schedule, I just couldn't keep my head above water. I feel terrible even saying that, because I should be able to care for my husband without making a mess of things, shouldn't I? I hope I don't sound like I'm complaining about him... it's just my own failing, really. Does that timeline help you at all?\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides your husband's health changes, have you experienced any changes in your own physical health, or have you been started on any new medications in the past year or so?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? I always try my very best to be a good listener, I really do. But... oh, it's a bit embarrassing to admit. I have noticed that if, say, the television is left on in the next room, or... or if I'm at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my mind just sort of... follows them instead. It just drifts right over to whatever they're doing. 

And then I completely lose my place in whatever I was writing or planning out. I feel so rude when it happens, because I really do want to focus and do a good job. I just get pulled away so easily by every little noise lately, no matter how hard I try to ignore it. Is that the sort of thing you meant? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of examples... I just want to be helpful to you.

I haven't had to start any new pills or anything like that in the past year. \n\nI know I'm getting a bit older, but my regular doctor says I'm doing quite well, really. I try very hard to take good care of myself so I can be useful to my husband and my church, you know? So... no, nothing new physically for me. I hope it's alright that I don't have anything to report there? I just want to be sure I'm giving you all the right details... am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: When you try to organize things or remember your husband's appointments, do you find that your memory itself isn't working as well as it used to, or is it more that you just feel overwhelmed by the amount of things to keep track of?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't think it's my memory, exactly. I mean, I can remember things just fine if I... if I really sit down and focus on them. It's not like I'm forgetting important things like my husband's name or our anniversary, thank goodness! \n\nIt's more just... well, yes, maybe overwhelmed is the right word. It's like there are all these little pieces of paper and dates floating around, and I just can't seem to grab hold of them and put them in the right boxes in my mind, if that makes sense? I'll look at the calendar and see three things written down, and my brain just sort of... flutters away to something else instead of dealing with it. I feel so silly saying it like that. I really should be able to just buckle down and do it. Does that sound like what you were asking? I always try my very best to be a good listener, I really do. But... oh, it's a bit embarrassing to admit. I have noticed that if, say, the television is left on in the next room, or... or if I'm at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my mind just sort of... follows them instead. Oh, well... I really don't want to sound like I'm just being lazy, I promise I'm not. But... it's not so much that I'm terribly worried about making a mistake. I usually know how to do the tasks. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I suppose it's really the wandering part once I finally do get started. I really do try my very best to focus on the task, I promise I do! But... oh, it's just so easy for my attention to slip away from me, even when I'm trying so hard to be good and get it done. 

Like, if I'm at the senior center trying to work on a schedule, and just two people start chatting nearby... or even at home, if the television happens to be on in the next room... my mind just sort of naturally follows their voices or the program instead. And then, before I even realize it's happened, I've completely lost my place in what I was writing or planning out. 

I always feel so silly when it happens. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly scatterbrained? I really am trying to do things right and be responsible. Did I explain that well enough for you? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.

I hope I don't sound like I'm complaining about him... it's just my own failing, really. My own health has been just fine, thankfully. I haven't had to start any new pills or anything like that in the past year. \n\nI know I'm getting a bit older, but my regular doctor says I'm doing quite well, really. I just want to be sure I'm giving you all the right details... am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: When you try to organize things or remember your husband's appointments, do you find that your memory itself isn't working as well as it used to, or is it more that you just feel overwhelmed by the amount of things to keep track of?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't think it's my memory, exactly. I mean, I can remember things just fine if I... if I really sit down and focus on them. It's not like I'm forgetting important things like my husband's name or our anniversary, thank goodness! \n\nIt's more just... well, yes, maybe overwhelmed is the right word. It's like there are all these little pieces of paper and dates floating around, and I just can't seem to grab hold of them and put them in the right boxes in my mind, if that makes sense? I'll look at the calendar and see three things written down, and my brain just sort of... flutters away to something else instead of dealing with it. I feel so silly saying it like that. And have you had other situations where you completely forgot something important that you had done or promised to do, only to remember it days or even weeks later?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, with the appointments... it wasn't days or weeks or anything dreadful like that! I promise I wouldn't let it go that far. Usually, I'd realize it maybe... oh, just the morning of the appointment, or perhaps the evening before when my husband would ask about it. It was just a little slip of the mind, you know? 

And as for forgetting other important things for days or weeks... oh, I don't think so. I really try to be so careful about important things. I mean, I might misplace my keys or forget to pick up milk, but I don't think I've ever just completely lost days and days of memory about something I promised to do. I would feel absolutely awful if I did that to someone! 

I really do my best to keep track of the big things, I promise. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I'm not giving you good enough examples? I just... I don't think I've had anything quite that severe happen. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... I really haven't noticed any particular time of day being worse than another. It just seems to stay about the same, really. 

I mean, I don't want you to think I'm just completely useless by dinnertime! I still get dinner on the table and everything. It's just... well, it's just this constant, silly little struggle to keep my thoughts together, whether it's ten in the morning or six at night. It doesn't seem to fluctuate or get terribly dramatic in the evening like you were asking. 

I always try my best to push through it, no matter what time it is. I wouldn't want my husband to think I was just giving up in the afternoons, you know? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for. I'm trying so hard to be accurate for you! Is that alright?

I just want to be helpful to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding the right words when you're speaking, or do you ever feel confused about dates, times, or where you are?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. And I don't have any trouble finding my words—well, except for right now, maybe, because I'm a little nervous! Thank goodness! I haven't had anything like that happen. No head injuries or... or bad fevers or anything scary like that. \n\nI try to take very good care of myself, you know. I'm just... well, just a bit disorganized sometimes, that's all. It's really just the focusing on things that I struggle with. I've never had a time where I was completely disoriented or lost like that. \n\nI would be absolutely terrified if that happened, and I certainly wouldn't hide it from you! I hope that's a good thing, that I haven't had that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of what's going on. I'm trying to be very clear for you, Doctor, is that helpful?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're trying to do something like fill out a form or organize papers, do you ever notice that it just takes you much longer than it used to, or that your thinking feels slower than it did before your husband's health changed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say my thinking feels slower. I mean, I certainly don't feel sluggish or anything like that! I still try to be very sharp. 

It might take me a tiny bit longer to fill out a form now, but I think that's just because I want to be extra careful and make sure I get all the details exactly right. You know how important those medical forms and bills are, especially now with my husband needing so much help. I just... I double-check things more, maybe? It's not that I can't understand them or that my brain is moving slowly. It's just that little bit of trouble keeping my focus steady while I'm doing it. 

I really try to stay on top of all the paperwork so my husband doesn't have to worry about it. I don't want you to think I'm just sitting there staring blankly at the page! I get it all done, I promise. It just takes a little more effort to keep my attention on the lines. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that right for you.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF48.9, F06.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to the clinic? What's been concerning you?\n\nPatient: Oh, hello Doctor. Thank you so much for having me. I, um, I hope I'm not taking up too much of your time with this. It's probably silly, but... well, I came in because I just can't seem to get anything in order anymore. \n\nIt's a bit embarrassing to admit, honestly. Like, my kitchen table is just covered right now with my husband's appointment papers, and church flyers, and notes from the senior center... oh, and some half-finished lists of mine. I sit there, meaning to sort it all out, but I just don't know which thing to do first. And then somehow the whole day is just gone. I feel awful about it, because I really should be able to manage a simple table, shouldn't I? Is that... is that the sort of thing I should be telling you?\n\nDoctor: Besides feeling frustrated about the clutter and organization, how has your mood been overall? Have you been feeling sad, down, or empty for any period of time recently?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. I wouldn't say I've been feeling sad, or... or empty. I mean, I have a wonderful husband and a lovely church community, so I really have absolutely no reason to be down. I try to stay quite cheerful, actually! \n\nIt's really just... well, just this silliness with not being able to keep my own house in order. I certainly don't want to give you the impression that I'm ungrateful or depressed. I'm just a little frustrated with myself, mostly, for being so scattered. I hope that makes sense? I want to make sure I'm giving you the right information... did I answer that okay?\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that you started having trouble organizing and keeping track of things like you're describing? Was it gradual, or did it come on more suddenly?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, I've always been a little bit of a daydreamer, maybe not the most perfectly tidy person in the world, but it was never really a bother. But it really started getting to be, well, a bit too much for me to handle about a year and three months ago. \n\nThat was when my husband's health changed a bit, and he started needing regular help keeping track of his appointments and all his daily arrangements. I wanted to do it all perfectly for him, of course, because he's been so good to me. But suddenly it was just... so many papers and dates, and that's when things really started piling up on the table. It wasn't overnight, but it felt like once I took on his schedule, I just couldn't keep my head above water. I feel terrible even saying that, because I should be able to care for my husband without making a mess of things, shouldn't I? I hope I don't sound like I'm complaining about him... it's just my own failing, really. Does that timeline help you at all?\n\nDoctor: Besides your husband's health changes, have you experienced any changes in your own physical health, or have you been started on any new medications in the past year or so?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. My own health has been just fine, thankfully. I haven't had to start any new pills or anything like that in the past year. \n\nI know I'm getting a bit older, but my regular doctor says I'm doing quite well, really. I try very hard to take good care of myself so I can be useful to my husband and my church, you know? So... no, nothing new physically for me. I hope it's alright that I don't have anything to report there? I just want to be sure I'm giving you all the right details... am I doing okay with these questions?\n\nDoctor: When you try to organize things or remember your husband's appointments, do you find that your memory itself isn't working as well as it used to, or is it more that you just feel overwhelmed by the amount of things to keep track of?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I don't think it's my memory, exactly. I mean, I can remember things just fine if I... if I really sit down and focus on them. It's not like I'm forgetting important things like my husband's name or our anniversary, thank goodness! \n\nIt's more just... well, yes, maybe overwhelmed is the right word. It's like there are all these little pieces of paper and dates floating around, and I just can't seem to grab hold of them and put them in the right boxes in my mind, if that makes sense? I'll look at the calendar and see three things written down, and my brain just sort of... flutters away to something else instead of dealing with it. I feel so silly saying it like that. I really should be able to just buckle down and do it. Does that sound like what you were asking? I want to make sure I'm explaining it properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or focus, even when you're not thinking about all those papers and appointments? For example, do you find it harder to follow along with conversations, or to read something and remember what you just read?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? I always try my very best to be a good listener, I really do. But... oh, it's a bit embarrassing to admit. I have noticed that if, say, the television is left on in the next room, or... or if I'm at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my mind just sort of... follows them instead. It just drifts right over to whatever they're doing. \n\nAnd then I completely lose my place in whatever I was writing or planning out. I feel so rude when it happens, because I really do want to focus and do a good job. I just get pulled away so easily by every little noise lately, no matter how hard I try to ignore it. Is that the sort of thing you meant? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of examples... I just want to be helpful to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding the right words when you're speaking, or do you ever feel confused about dates, times, or where you are?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. Nothing like that at all. I might get a little distracted, like I said, but I always know where I am and what day it is. And I don't have any trouble finding my words—well, except for right now, maybe, because I'm a little nervous! But normally, no, I can speak just fine. \n\nI'm very careful to keep my mind sharp, you know. I do the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper every week, and I read my Bible every morning. I promise I'm not getting confused or... or anything like that. I would tell you if I was, I really would. I know how important it is to be honest with you. Did I answer that okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need to know.\n\nDoctor: In the past year or so, have you had any head injuries, high fevers, infections, or any other serious medical events—even if they seemed minor at the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. Thank goodness! I haven't had anything like that happen. No head injuries or... or bad fevers or anything scary like that. \n\nI try to take very good care of myself, you know. I keep up with all my checkups and I'm very careful not to fall or get sick. I hope that's a good thing to hear? I'd hate for you to worry that something terrible had happened to me physically. I'm really quite healthy in that regard, I promise. Did I cover everything you were asking about there? I can try to think back harder if you need me to!\n\nDoctor: When you think about organizing things or managing all those papers and appointments, do you find yourself feeling quite anxious or worried about doing it wrong, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started or stay focused on the task itself?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really don't want to sound like I'm just being lazy, I promise I'm not. But... it's not so much that I'm terribly worried about making a mistake. I usually know how to do the tasks. It's more that... well, when there’s a form to fill out, or those long insurance papers to read through, or even just putting together a program schedule for the ladies' group... I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. \n\nIt's a bit embarrassing to say, but it's like my head just feels completely worn out before I even start. I'll look at the paperwork and suddenly decide I absolutely must go water the plants or wipe down the counters right that very minute, just to avoid sitting down with it. I feel terrible admitting that, because I know I should just sit down and get it done like a responsible person. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right for you... I really want to make sure I'm answering your question properly.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down to work on organizing those papers, is the main problem that you just don't feel like starting, or is it that once you do start, you find it very hard to keep your attention on the task because your mind keeps wandering?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose it's really the wandering part once I finally do get started. I really do try my very best to focus on the task, I promise I do! But... oh, it's just so easy for my attention to slip away from me, even when I'm trying so hard to be good and get it done. \n\nLike, if I'm at the senior center trying to work on a schedule, and just two people start chatting nearby... or even at home, if the television happens to be on in the next room... my mind just sort of naturally follows their voices or the program instead. And then, before I even realize it's happened, I've completely lost my place in what I was writing or planning out. \n\nI always feel so silly when it happens. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly scatterbrained? I really am trying to do things right and be responsible. Did I explain that well enough for you? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can remember things fine when you focus—like your husband's name and your anniversary. But over this past year, have you noticed that you forget things you normally would remember easily, even when you're not distracted? For example, forgetting why you walked into a room, or forgetting something someone just told you a few minutes ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really hope this doesn't make me sound like I'm losing my mind or anything, because I promise I'm not. But... yes, I suppose I have been forgetting some everyday things a bit more lately. \n\nIt's just little things, mostly! Like... oh, I'll mean to return a phone call and then just completely forget about it. Or... well, sometimes I'll leave a bill sitting under a stack of mail and completely forget it's there until it's late. And... I hate to admit this, because I always want to be helpful, but I've forgotten a few little errands I promised my daughter I would run for her. \n\nThe worst one, though... and I felt just terrible about this... more than once, I've realized entirely too late that I never actually called to confirm one of my husband's appointments. I felt so bad about that, I really did. I know I should be more on top of things. I hope you don't think I'm just being careless? I really do try to keep everything straight. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I can try to think of other things if you need me to!\n\nDoctor: You mentioned forgetting to call to confirm your husband's appointments—when that happened, how long after you were supposed to call did you realize you had forgotten? And have you had other situations where you completely forgot something important that you had done or promised to do, only to remember it days or even weeks later?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, with the appointments... it wasn't days or weeks or anything dreadful like that! I promise I wouldn't let it go that far. Usually, I'd realize it maybe... oh, just the morning of the appointment, or perhaps the evening before when my husband would ask about it. It was just a little slip of the mind, you know? \n\nAnd as for forgetting other important things for days or weeks... oh, I don't think so. I really try to be so careful about important things. I mean, I might misplace my keys or forget to pick up milk, but I don't think I've ever just completely lost days and days of memory about something I promised to do. I would feel absolutely awful if I did that to someone! \n\nI really do my best to keep track of the big things, I promise. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I'm not giving you good enough examples? I just... I don't think I've had anything quite that severe happen. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: In the past year or so, have you had any infections—like a urinary tract infection, pneumonia, or anything like that—even if it seemed minor or you treated it at home? Sometimes infections can affect how we think and concentrate.\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... no infections like that at all. I've been very lucky with my health in that regard, I really have. I try to take very good care of myself, you know, drinking plenty of water and taking my vitamins every morning so I don't get sick and become a burden to anyone. \n\nI haven't had pneumonia or a urinary tract infection or... well, anything like that in the past year. Just completely healthy in that way! I promise I'd tell you if I had, I know how important it is to be honest with doctors. But no, nothing like that. I hope that's an okay answer? I know you're trying to figure out what's causing all this silliness with my memory and attention, and I want to be as helpful as I possibly can be.\n\nDoctor: Throughout the day, do you notice that your concentration and ability to focus on tasks gets worse at certain times—like maybe in the evening or late afternoon—or does it stay pretty much the same all day long?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really haven't noticed any particular time of day being worse than another. It just seems to stay about the same, really. \n\nI mean, I don't want you to think I'm just completely useless by dinnertime! I still get dinner on the table and everything. It's just... well, it's just this constant, silly little struggle to keep my thoughts together, whether it's ten in the morning or six at night. It doesn't seem to fluctuate or get terribly dramatic in the evening like you were asking. \n\nI always try my best to push through it, no matter what time it is. I wouldn't want my husband to think I was just giving up in the afternoons, you know? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for. I'm trying so hard to be accurate for you! Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I understand the timeline correctly. You said this all started about a year and three months ago when your husband's health changed. But looking back, was there ever a time—even just a few days or a week—when you suddenly felt very confused or disoriented, like you weren't quite sure where you were or what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean... being confused about where I am or what's happening? That sounds so frightening! I promise you, I've never had anything like that happen. \n\nI know I get a bit scattered, and I forget things, but I always know perfectly well where I am and who I'm with. I'm trying to be very clear for you, Doctor, is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something like fill out a form or organize papers, do you ever notice that it just takes you much longer than it used to, or that your thinking feels slower than it did before your husband's health changed?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say my thinking feels slower. But... oh, it's just so easy for my attention to slip away from me, even when I'm trying so hard to be good and get it done. \n\nLike, if I'm at the senior center trying to work on a schedule, and just two people start chatting nearby... or even at home, if the television happens to be on in the next room... my mind just sort of naturally follows their voices or the program instead. And then, before I even realize it's happened, I've completely lost my place in what I was writing or planning out. \n\nI always feel so silly when it happens. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly scatterbrained? I really am trying to do things right and be responsible. Did I explain that well enough for you? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can remember things fine when you focus—like your husband's name and your anniversary. But over this past year, have you noticed that you forget things you normally would remember easily, even when you're not distracted? For example, forgetting why you walked into a room, or forgetting something someone just told you a few minutes ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really hope this doesn't make me sound like I'm losing my mind or anything, because I promise I'm not. I felt so bad about that, I really did. I know I should be more on top of things. I hope you don't think I'm just being careless? I really do try to keep everything straight. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about? It was just a little slip of the mind, you know? \n\nAnd as for forgetting other important things for days or weeks... oh, I don't think so. I really try to be so careful about important things. I mean, I might misplace my keys or forget to pick up milk, but I don't think I've ever just completely lost days and days of memory about something I promised to do. I would feel absolutely awful if I did that to someone! \n\nI really do my best to keep track of the big things, I promise. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: In the past year or so, have you had any infections—like a urinary tract infection, pneumonia, or anything like that—even if it seemed minor or you treated it at home? I try to take very good care of myself, you know, drinking plenty of water and taking my vitamins every morning so I don't get sick and become a burden to anyone. \n\nI haven't had pneumonia or a urinary tract infection or... well, anything like that in the past year. I want to make sure I'm explaining it properly for you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to concentrate or focus, even when you're not thinking about all those papers and appointments? For example, do you find it harder to follow along with conversations, or to read something and remember what you just read?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... maybe a little bit? I always try my very best to be a good listener, I really do. But... oh, it's a bit embarrassing to admit. I have noticed that if, say, the television is left on in the next room, or... or if I'm at the senior center and two people start talking near me, my mind just sort of... follows them instead. It just drifts right over to whatever they're doing. \n\nAnd then I completely lose my place in whatever I was writing or planning out. I feel so rude when it happens, because I really do want to focus and do a good job. I just get pulled away so easily by every little noise lately, no matter how hard I try to ignore it. Is that the sort of thing you meant? I hope I'm giving you the right kind of examples... I just want to be helpful to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty finding the right words when you're speaking, or do you ever feel confused about dates, times, or where you are?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. Nothing like that at all. I might get a little distracted, like I said, but I always know where I am and what day it is. And I don't have any trouble finding my words—well, except for right now, maybe, because I'm a little nervous! But normally, no, I can speak just fine. \n\nI'm very careful to keep my mind sharp, you know. I do the crossword puzzles in the Sunday paper every week, and I read my Bible every morning. I promise I'm not getting confused or... or anything like that. I would tell you if I was, I really would. I know how important it is to be honest with you. Did I answer that okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need to know.\n\nDoctor: In the past year or so, have you had any head injuries, high fevers, infections, or any other serious medical events—even if they seemed minor at the time?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. Thank goodness! I haven't had anything like that happen. No head injuries or... or bad fevers or anything scary like that. \n\nI try to take very good care of myself, you know. I keep up with all my checkups and I'm very careful not to fall or get sick. I hope that's a good thing to hear? I'd hate for you to worry that something terrible had happened to me physically. I'm really quite healthy in that regard, I promise. Did I cover everything you were asking about there? I can try to think back harder if you need me to!\n\nDoctor: When you think about organizing things or managing all those papers and appointments, do you find yourself feeling quite anxious or worried about doing it wrong, or is it more that you just can't seem to get started or stay focused on the task itself?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really don't want to sound like I'm just being lazy, I promise I'm not. But... it's not so much that I'm terribly worried about making a mistake. I usually know how to do the tasks. It's more that... well, when there’s a form to fill out, or those long insurance papers to read through, or even just putting together a program schedule for the ladies' group... I just keep finding little reasons to put it off. \n\nIt's a bit embarrassing to say, but it's like my head just feels completely worn out before I even start. I'll look at the paperwork and suddenly decide I absolutely must go water the plants or wipe down the counters right that very minute, just to avoid sitting down with it. I feel terrible admitting that, because I know I should just sit down and get it done like a responsible person. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right for you... I really want to make sure I'm answering your question properly.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting down to work on organizing those papers, is the main problem that you just don't feel like starting, or is it that once you do start, you find it very hard to keep your attention on the task because your mind keeps wandering?\n\nPatient: Well... I suppose it's really the wandering part once I finally do get started. I really do try my very best to focus on the task, I promise I do! But... oh, it's just so easy for my attention to slip away from me, even when I'm trying so hard to be good and get it done. \n\nLike, if I'm at the senior center trying to work on a schedule, and just two people start chatting nearby... or even at home, if the television happens to be on in the next room... my mind just sort of naturally follows their voices or the program instead. And then, before I even realize it's happened, I've completely lost my place in what I was writing or planning out. \n\nI always feel so silly when it happens. I hope that doesn't make me sound terribly scatterbrained? I really am trying to do things right and be responsible. Did I explain that well enough for you? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right kind of examples.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can remember things fine when you focus—like your husband's name and your anniversary. But over this past year, have you noticed that you forget things you normally would remember easily, even when you're not distracted? For example, forgetting why you walked into a room, or forgetting something someone just told you a few minutes ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really hope this doesn't make me sound like I'm losing my mind or anything, because I promise I'm not. But... yes, I suppose I have been forgetting some everyday things a bit more lately. \n\nIt's just little things, mostly! Like... oh, I'll mean to return a phone call and then just completely forget about it. Or... well, sometimes I'll leave a bill sitting under a stack of mail and completely forget it's there until it's late. And... I hate to admit this, because I always want to be helpful, but I've forgotten a few little errands I promised my daughter I would run for her. \n\nThe worst one, though... and I felt just terrible about this... more than once, I've realized entirely too late that I never actually called to confirm one of my husband's appointments. I felt so bad about that, I really did. I know I should be more on top of things. I hope you don't think I'm just being careless? I really do try to keep everything straight. Is that the kind of thing you were asking about? I can try to think of other things if you need me to!\n\nDoctor: You mentioned forgetting to call to confirm your husband's appointments—when that happened, how long after you were supposed to call did you realize you had forgotten? And have you had other situations where you completely forgot something important that you had done or promised to do, only to remember it days or even weeks later?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, with the appointments... it wasn't days or weeks or anything dreadful like that! I promise I wouldn't let it go that far. Usually, I'd realize it maybe... oh, just the morning of the appointment, or perhaps the evening before when my husband would ask about it. It was just a little slip of the mind, you know? \n\nAnd as for forgetting other important things for days or weeks... oh, I don't think so. I really try to be so careful about important things. I mean, I might misplace my keys or forget to pick up milk, but I don't think I've ever just completely lost days and days of memory about something I promised to do. I would feel absolutely awful if I did that to someone! \n\nI really do my best to keep track of the big things, I promise. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I'm not giving you good enough examples? I just... I don't think I've had anything quite that severe happen. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: In the past year or so, have you had any infections—like a urinary tract infection, pneumonia, or anything like that—even if it seemed minor or you treated it at home? Sometimes infections can affect how we think and concentrate.\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... no infections like that at all. I've been very lucky with my health in that regard, I really have. I try to take very good care of myself, you know, drinking plenty of water and taking my vitamins every morning so I don't get sick and become a burden to anyone. \n\nI haven't had pneumonia or a urinary tract infection or... well, anything like that in the past year. Just completely healthy in that way! I promise I'd tell you if I had, I know how important it is to be honest with doctors. But no, nothing like that. I hope that's an okay answer? I know you're trying to figure out what's causing all this silliness with my memory and attention, and I want to be as helpful as I possibly can be.\n\nDoctor: Throughout the day, do you notice that your concentration and ability to focus on tasks gets worse at certain times—like maybe in the evening or late afternoon—or does it stay pretty much the same all day long?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really haven't noticed any particular time of day being worse than another. It just seems to stay about the same, really. \n\nI mean, I don't want you to think I'm just completely useless by dinnertime! I still get dinner on the table and everything. It's just... well, it's just this constant, silly little struggle to keep my thoughts together, whether it's ten in the morning or six at night. It doesn't seem to fluctuate or get terribly dramatic in the evening like you were asking. \n\nI always try my best to push through it, no matter what time it is. I wouldn't want my husband to think I was just giving up in the afternoons, you know? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for. I'm trying so hard to be accurate for you! Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I understand the timeline correctly. You said this all started about a year and three months ago when your husband's health changed. But looking back, was there ever a time—even just a few days or a week—when you suddenly felt very confused or disoriented, like you weren't quite sure where you were or what was happening around you?\n\nPatient: Oh, my goodness, no. No, nothing like that at all. I mean... being confused about where I am or what's happening? That sounds so frightening! I promise you, I've never had anything like that happen. \n\nI know I get a bit scattered, and I forget things, but I always know perfectly well where I am and who I'm with. I'm just... well, just a bit disorganized sometimes, that's all. It's really just the focusing on things that I struggle with. I've never had a time where I was completely disoriented or lost like that. \n\nI would be absolutely terrified if that happened, and I certainly wouldn't hide it from you! I hope that's a good thing, that I haven't had that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of what's going on. I'm trying to be very clear for you, Doctor, is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something like fill out a form or organize papers, do you ever notice that it just takes you much longer than it used to, or that your thinking feels slower than it did before your husband's health changed?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say my thinking feels slower. I mean, I certainly don't feel sluggish or anything like that! I still try to be very sharp. \n\nIt might take me a tiny bit longer to fill out a form now, but I think that's just because I want to be extra careful and make sure I get all the details exactly right. You know how important those medical forms and bills are, especially now with my husband needing so much help. I just... I double-check things more, maybe? It's not that I can't understand them or that my brain is moving slowly. It's just that little bit of trouble keeping my focus steady while I'm doing it. \n\nI really try to stay on top of all the paperwork so my husband doesn't have to worry about it. I don't want you to think I'm just sitting there staring blankly at the page! I get it all done, I promise. It just takes a little more effort to keep my attention on the lines. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining that right for you.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone—your husband, your daughter, or someone else close to you—ever mentioned that you seemed confused or 'not yourself' during this past year, even if just for a few hours or a day? Sometimes other people notice changes that we don't see in ourselves.\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. No, neither of them have ever said anything like that to me. I'm sure they would tell me if I was acting strangely or seemed confused! \n\nI mean, my daughter sometimes teases me a little if I misplace my glasses or if I ask her the same question twice, but she just laughs it off. She knows I've got a lot on my plate right now with her father. And my husband... well, he's never said I seem like I'm not myself. I always try to be very cheerful and capable around him, so he doesn't have to worry about me on top of everything else. \n\nI'd be so embarrassed if I was walking around acting confused and everyone else noticed but me! But no, I promise you, nobody has ever mentioned anything like that. I'm still very much myself, just... maybe a little more easily distracted these days. I hope that puts your mind at ease about that? I really want to be sure I'm giving you all the right answers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I promise I wouldn't let it go that far. Usually, I'd realize it maybe... oh, just the morning of the appointment, or perhaps the evening before when my husband would ask about it. It was just a little slip of the mind, you know? \n\nAnd as for forgetting other important things for days or weeks... oh, I don't think so. I really try to be so careful about important things. I mean, I might misplace my keys or forget to pick up milk, but I don't think I've ever just completely lost days and days of memory about something I promised to do. I would feel absolutely awful if I did that to someone! \n\nI really do my best to keep track of the big things, I promise. I hope that doesn't make me sound like I'm not giving you good enough examples? I just... I don't think I've had anything quite that severe happen. Does that help answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: In the past year or so, have you had any infections—like a urinary tract infection, pneumonia, or anything like that—even if it seemed minor or you treated it at home? Sometimes infections can affect how we think and concentrate.\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no... no infections like that at all. I've been very lucky with my health in that regard, I really have. I try to take very good care of myself, you know, drinking plenty of water and taking my vitamins every morning so I don't get sick and become a burden to anyone. \n\nI haven't had pneumonia or a urinary tract infection or... well, anything like that in the past year. Just completely healthy in that way! I promise I'd tell you if I had, I know how important it is to be honest with doctors. But no, nothing like that. I hope that's an okay answer? I know you're trying to figure out what's causing all this silliness with my memory and attention, and I want to be as helpful as I possibly can be.\n\nDoctor: Throughout the day, do you notice that your concentration and ability to focus on tasks gets worse at certain times—like maybe in the evening or late afternoon—or does it stay pretty much the same all day long?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I really haven't noticed any particular time of day being worse than another. It just seems to stay about the same, really. \n\nI mean, I don't want you to think I'm just completely useless by dinnertime! I still get dinner on the table and everything. It's just... well, it's just this constant, silly little struggle to keep my thoughts together, whether it's ten in the morning or six at night. It doesn't seem to fluctuate or get terribly dramatic in the evening like you were asking. \n\nI always try my best to push through it, no matter what time it is. I wouldn't want my husband to think I was just giving up in the afternoons, you know? I hope that's the kind of information you were looking for. I'm trying so hard to be accurate for you! Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: I want to make sure I understand the timeline correctly. You said this all started about a year and three months ago when your husband's health changed. I'm just... well, just a bit disorganized sometimes, that's all. It's really just the focusing on things that I struggle with. I've never had a time where I was completely disoriented or lost like that. \n\nI would be absolutely terrified if that happened, and I certainly wouldn't hide it from you! I hope that's a good thing, that I haven't had that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture of what's going on. I'm trying to be very clear for you, Doctor, is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something like fill out a form or organize papers, do you ever notice that it just takes you much longer than it used to, or that your thinking feels slower than it did before your husband's health changed?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I wouldn't say my thinking feels slower. I always try to be very cheerful and capable around him, so he doesn't have to worry about me on top of everything else. \n\nI'd be so embarrassed if I was walking around acting confused and everyone else noticed but me! I really want to be sure I'm giving you all the right answers.\n\nDoctor: When you think about sitting down to organize those papers or handle your husband's appointments, do you notice that you're actually avoiding it because it feels overwhelming or stressful to you—like part of you just doesn't want to deal with it—or does it feel more like your brain just physically can't stay focused on the task no matter how much you want to?\n\nPatient: Well... oh dear, I suppose... Well... yes, I do get it done! I always make sure it gets finished. I mean, I really try my absolute hardest when I sit down. I'll tell myself, "Okay, just focus, get this one page done," and...

Oh, dear... well, I do try my very best to keep my eyes right on the page. I really do. But... I suppose... if there's any little noise around me, it gets quite difficult to stay with it. 

Like, if my husband has the television on in the next room, or... or if I'm at the senior center trying to write out my planning list and two people start talking near me... my mind just sort of follows their conversation instead. I certainly don't mean to eavesdrop, I promise! It's just that my brain goes right over to what they're saying or what's on the screen, and then I completely lose my place in what I was writing or reading. 

I always feel so foolish when it happens, having to start the whole paragraph over again. I hope you don't think I'm just making excuses or not taking it seriously? I really am trying to be a good wife and get it all done properly. I just get pulled away by things so easily. Does that answer your question alright?
